On De Giorgi’s conjecture of nonlocal approximations for free-discontinuity problems: The symmetric gradient case
Authors: S. Almi, E. Davoli, A. Kubin, E. Tasso
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We prove that E. De Giorgi’s conjecture for the nonlocal approximation of free-discontinuity problems extends to the case of functionals defined in terms of the symmetric gradient of the admissible field. After introducing a suitable class of continuous finite-difference approximants, we show the compactness of deformations with equibounded energies, as well as their Gamma-convergence. The compactness analysis is a crucial hurdle, which we overcome by generalizing a Fréchet-Kolmogorov approach previously introduced by two of the authors. A second essential difficulty is the identification of the limiting space of admissible deformations, since a control on the directional variations is, a priori, only available in average. A limiting representation in GSBD is eventually established via a novel characterization of this space.

Introduction

Free-discontinuity problems and their approximation by means of Sobolev formulations, discrete descriptions, or nonlocal functionals are a thriving research area owing to their broad scope of applications, ranging from image reconstruction, to the modeling of failure phenomena in continuum mechanics. A milestone in this direction was a conjecture formulated by E. De Giorgi and proven by M. Gobbino [31], dealing with the approximation via \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-convergence of the Mumford-Shah functional by a sequence of nonlocal counterparts. Such analysis has then paved the way for further generalizations in [22, 32]. All the aforementioned contributions deal with free-discontinuity problems involving the full distributional gradient of the admissible maps. A question that, to the authors’ knowledge, was so far left open, was whether similar nonlocal approximation techniques would also prove successful for the study of free-boundary problems involving more general differential operators.

In this paper we initiate the study of continuous finite-difference approximations of linearized Griffith functionals. Aside from purely mathematical interest, such inquiries are deeply rooted in the recent research lines which are developing in image processing and data science. Motivated by applications in Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) or Positron Emission Tomography (PET), regularizers involving more general differential operators than the gradient have been studied, e.g., in the settings of regularization graphs [9], for higher-order Total Variation operators [10, 12], as well as in structural Total Variation approaches [33] (see also [13] for a review on data-driven approaches in image regularization). Concerning the data-science applicative interest, starting from the variational analysis in [30], a novel research direction has open up for variational studies on point clouds (see, e.g., [6, 15, 16, 24]), as well as related machine-learning applications [14]. The corresponding mathematical study of generalized graph-based differential constraints different from curl \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=0$$\end{document}A=0 is, to the authors’ knowledge, a thriving, mostly unexplored, subject (see, e.g., [34] and the references therein).

The case study we tackle here is a symmetrized counterpart of the nonlocal energies considered in [31, 32]. In order to describe our findings, we first recall the results [31], where the sequence of functionals1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathscr {F}_{\varepsilon }(u,\Omega ) :=\frac{1}{\varepsilon ^{n+1}}\int _{\Omega \times \Omega } \arctan \left( \frac{( u( x' )-u( x ))^2}{\varepsilon } \right) e^{-|\frac{ x' - [4, Section 4.5]. Note that the multiplicative constants in (1.2) depend only on the choice of the integrand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\arctan (x)$$\end{document}arctan(x) and on the weight function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-|x|^2}$$\end{document}e-|x|2.

It is thus natural, in a first stage, to ask whether the functional1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {F}_{\varepsilon }(u,\Omega ) :=\frac{1}{\varepsilon ^{n+1}}\int _{\Omega \times \Omega } \arctan \left( \frac{( (u( x' )-u( x )) \cdot (x' - x) )^2}{\varepsilon ^3} \right) e^{-|\frac{ x' - x }{\varepsilon }|^2} \text {d}x' \text {d}x \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Fε(u,Ω):=1εn+1∫Ω×Ωarctan((u(x′)-u(x))·(x′-x))2ε3e-|x′-xε|2dx′dxdefined for measurable functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in L^{0} (\Omega ; In the expression above, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GSBD}(\Omega )$$\end{document}GSBD(Ω) denotes the space of functions with generalized special bounded deformation (see [26]), and e(u) the absolutely continuous part of the symmetric distributional gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}(u):=\tfrac{Du+Du^{\top }}{2}$$\end{document}E(u):=Du+Du⊤2.

The structure of (1.3) aligns with the principles of linearized theories in continuum mechanics, where only the symmetric part of the gradient contributes to the deformation energy. This consideration naturally motivates the assumption that only the component of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(x') - u(x)$$\end{document}u(x′)-u(x) projected along the direction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x'-x$$\end{document}x′-x should provide energy contributions. By setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \xi :=x' -x$$\end{document}εξ:=x′-x, when the differences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x + \varepsilon \xi ) - u(x)) \cdot \xi $$\end{document}(u(x+εξ)-u(x))·ξ are relatively small, the functionals in (1.3) behave like pure bulk energies. For large values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x + \varepsilon \xi ) - u(x)) \cdot \xi $$\end{document}(u(x+εξ)-u(x))·ξ, instead, the energies in (1.3) saturate, effectively detecting and penalizing the size of the discontinuities of u.

An important remark concerns the fact that the energies in (1.1) only approximate the Mumford-Shah functional under suitable additional assumptions on the geometry of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω, such as Lipschitz regularity (see [31, Remark 7.1]). Such requirements may not be fully consistent when handling free-boundary problems where discontinuities are already present within the material. Consider, for example, the case of a crack-initiated domain, where the presence of an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n-1)$$\end{document}(n-1)-dimensional set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma \subset \Omega $$\end{document}Γ⊂Ω, representing an initial crack makes the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \setminus \Gamma $$\end{document}Ω\Γ not Lipschitz.

This possible lack of regularity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω calls for a more refined approach, which we address by modifying the energies in (1.3) as follows. The major difficulty we face in the analysis of (1.4) is the lack of a clear compactness strategy. The results of [3, 19, 20] cannot be applied, as the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{p}_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Fεp is defined over the space of measurable maps, so that neither a differential structure nor integrability assumptions are given a priori. Thus, our main result is the following compactness and closure theorem for sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}uε with equibounded energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{p}_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Fεp, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in [1, +\infty )$$\end{document}p∈[1,+∞).

\setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _p$$\end{document}βp is a positive constant. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=1$$\end{document}p=1 we have the following explicit form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {F}^1(u,\Omega )= \frac{\pi ^{\frac{n}{2}}}{2}\int _{\Omega } \Big (|e(u)|^2+\frac{1}{2}\text {div}(u)^2\Big )\,\text {d}x +\frac{\pi ^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}{2}\mathcal {H}^{n-1}(J_u), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F1(u,Ω)=πn22∫Ω(|e(u)|2+12div(u)2)dx+πn+122Hn-1(Ju),whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in GSBD (\Omega )$$\end{document}u∈GSBD(Ω).

For the precise definition of the bulk energy density \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _p$$\end{document}φp and of the surface energy density \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _p$$\end{document}βp we refer to Lemma 4.6.

In proving Theorem 1.1 we addressed two main correlated hurdles. The first difficulty concerns the identification of a limit map u. In this respect, we may compare our setting to the existing literature concerning nonlocal approximation of Free Discontinuity problems. In particular, the recent works [28, 35, 39], inspired by the corresponding results on the Mumford-Shah functional [7, 36], provide a nonlocal approximation of a class of Griffith-type functionals in terms of a bulk energy of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{\varepsilon } \int _{\Omega } f \big ( \varepsilon W(e(u)) * \rho _{\varepsilon }\big )\, \textrm{d}x \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1ε∫Ωf(εW(e(u))∗ρε)dxfeaturing the convolution of a volume density W(e(u)) for a Sobolev map u and a potential f whose behavior is similar to that of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\arctan $$\end{document}arctan in (1.3). The compactness issues in the mentioned articles are overcome due to the presence of an a priori differential structure of the displacement u, relying on compactness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GSBD}$$\end{document}GSBD [19, 20]. We remark that such a direct approach is not applicable in our framework, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}uε are only measurable fields with no control on their symmetric gradient. A similar issue appeared in a discrete fashion in [1, 23] on a deterministic lattice, which indeed proposed a discrete finite-difference approximation of Griffith-type of energies in the spirit of [5, 17, 18, 38] with a finite range of interaction. Their approach to compactness builds upon the construction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GSBD}$$\end{document}GSBD-competitors with uniformly bounded energy, which is facilitated by the lack of long-range interactions. This approach allowed again for the application of compactness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GSBD}$$\end{document}GSBD. The continuous nature of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{p}_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Fεp and its infinite horizon make the adaptation of the strategy of [23] rather challenging.

It turned out that in our setting we can work directly on the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}uε, bypassing the modification approach of [23]. Our method relies on Fréchet-Kolmogorov, and thus amounts to prove equi-continuity of translations. This technical step is provided in the proof of Theorem 1.1 and shares common ideas with [3], where the proof of the compactness theorem of [19] was first revisited, avoiding the use of Korn and Korn-Poincaré inequalities. Once again, the lack of a symmetric gradient and the structure of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Fε, which features an extra integration over the directions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi \in \mathbb {R}^{n}$$\end{document}ξ∈Rn, do not allow us to select a preferred basis to control translations, as it was the case in [3]. Nevertheless, the slicing properties of the functionals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Fε make it possible to control the translations of the maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\arctan (u_{\varepsilon }(x)\cdot \xi )$$\end{document}arctan(uε(x)·ξ) both with respect to x and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ. Pointwise convergence out of an exceptional set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A{\subset } \Omega $$\end{document}A⊂Ω is thus recovered by Fréchet-Kolmogorov Theorem.

The next step in the proof of Theorem 1.1 consists in showing that u belongs to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GSBD}(\Omega )$$\end{document}GSBD(Ω), yielding the characterization of the domain of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-limit of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{p}_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Fεp. Broadly speaking, the non-local nature of the approximating functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^p_\varepsilon $$\end{document}Fεp results in a limiting function space consisting of measurable vector fields that exhibit generalized bounded deformation in a weaker sense. To explain this phenomenon more precisely, recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in \textrm{GBD}(\Omega )$$\end{document}u∈GBD(Ω) if and only if u is a measurable vector field and there exists a finite Radon measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω such that, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi \in \mathbb {S}^{n-1}$$\end{document}ξ∈Sn-1, the generalized directional variation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mu }_u^\xi $$\end{document}μ^uξ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \cdot \xi $$\end{document}u·ξ, as introduced in [26, Definition 4.10], satisfies:1.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\mu }^\xi _u(B) \le \lambda (B), \quad \text {for every Borel set } B \subset \Omega . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ^uξ(B)≤λ(B),for every Borel setB⊂Ω.By a slicing argument, a similar approach to that proposed by M. Gobbino in [31] gives that the limit field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in L^{0} (\Omega ; \mathbb {R}^{n})$$\end{document}u∈L0(Ω;Rn) satisfies condition (1.8) in an averaged form with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ, namely,1.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {S}^{n-1}} \hat{\mu }^\xi _u(B) \, \textrm{d}\mathcal {H}^{n-1}(\xi ) \le \lambda (B), \quad \text {for every Borel set } B \subset \Omega . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Sn-1μ^uξ(B)dHn-1(ξ)≤λ(B),for every Borel setB⊂Ω.The fact that (1.8) is replaced in our setting by (1.9) is a huge obstacle to establish the structural properties of the limiting function space. In particular, the technique presented in [26] for deriving the GBD-structural properties fails, and we need to rely on the more recent integral geometric approach introduced in [2]. The main difference from the approach in [2] lies in the role of the flatness of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Rn, where rescaling and translating the domain preserve straight lines as geodesics, maintaining invariance in the associated GBD-space. Furthermore, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GBD}$$\end{document}GBD-theory developed in [25] provides a quite general method to relate the jump set of u to the behavior of codimension one slices, which we are able to adapt to our setting. We remark that such an approach seems not to be successful in a Riemannian manifold. In Propositions 3.15–3.17, we leverage the above features to avoid relying on a Korn-Poincaré-type inequality to estimate the dimensionality of the set on which the jump of all one-dimensional slices of u concentrate. Ultimately, we conclude in Theorem 3.1 that the limit map u belongs to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GSBD}(\Omega )$$\end{document}GSBD(Ω), and thus characterize the domain of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-limit of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{p}_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Fεp. It is worth noting that, although it is not used in the present paper, this method also allows one to address the case of jump sets with infinite length, which may be of interest for cohesive models obtained as the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-limit of finite-difference approximating functionals, as in [32]. For the detailed definitions of the relevant quantities and the proof of the structural properties, we refer the reader to Sections 2 and 3, respectively.

An alternative approach.

After the first version of this paper was submitted as a Preprint on arXiv, a new characterization of GSBD with finite jump was proved in [21], from which it was deduced that our limiting space is GSBD. This characterization was based on controlling the measures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mu }^\xi _u$$\end{document}μ^uξ through finitely many direction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ, which turns out to be equivalent to a control on average. In this new version we propose a totally different perspective, by first deriving fine properties of the limiting space and then proving it coincides with GSBD via some integro-geometrical arguments, hence providing an alternative proof to [21].

Eventually, we obtain the following \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-convergence result for the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{p}_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Fεp (see Definition 2.1).

Theorem 1.2

(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-convergence) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subset \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Ω⊂Rn be open. For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\ge 1$$\end{document}p≥1 the family of functionals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {F}^p_\varepsilon \}_{\varepsilon >0}$$\end{document}{Fεp}ε>0 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-converges in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^0(\Omega ;\mathbb {R}^n)$$\end{document}L0(Ω;Rn) to the Griffith-type functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^p$$\end{document}Fp defined as1.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {F}^{p} (u, \Omega ):= {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \displaystyle \int _{\Omega } \varphi _{p} (e(u)) \text {d}x + \beta _{p} \mathcal {H}^{n-1}(J_u)& \text {for }u \in \textrm{GSBD}^2(\Omega ),\\ \displaystyle \infty & \text {otherwise in }L^{0} (\Omega ; \mathbb {R}^{n}), \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Fp(u,Ω):=∫Ωφp(e(u))dx+βpHn-1(Ju)foru∈GSBD2(Ω),∞otherwise inL0(Ω;Rn),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _{p} $$\end{document}φp and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _{p}$$\end{document}βp are given as in Theorem 1.1.

Combining Theorem 1.1 with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-convergence of Theorem 1.2, we further deduce convergence of minimisers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_{\varepsilon }^p$$\end{document}Fεp to minimisers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^p$$\end{document}Fp under suitable Dirichlet boundary conditions (cf. Theorem 5.2).

Organization of the paper. The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we collect a few preliminary definitions and results. Section 3 is devoted to establish the main properties of the spaces of limiting deformations. Sections 4 and 5 are devoted to the proofs of our compactness, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-convergence result, respectively.

Outlook. The analysis provided in this work offers the opportunity to explore several further research lines. On the one hand, the compactness argument of Theorem 1.1 and the slicing technique of Theorem 1.2 may be further tested against discrete finite-difference approximations on point clouds of vectorial free discontinuity problems, in the spirit of [8, 16, 30] for the Total Variation, the Mumford-Shah, and the perimeter functionals. Such research line is the subject of a preprint which is currently in preparation.

Moreover, the blow-up strategy developed in the proof of Lemma 4.6 appears flexible enough to address nonhomogeneous energies, namely functionals with x-dependent bulk densities. In this setting, no additional obstruction arises at the level of compactness, as soon as the inhomogeneous bulk part is controlled from below by the corresponding homogeneous density. Under this assumption, the compactness argument carries over without substantial modifications, and the localization underlying the blow-up procedure remains applicable.

On the other hand, the use of a slicing argument for the study of the variational limit restricts the choice of the approximating sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Fε, which in turn determines the class of admissible densities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _{p}$$\end{document}φp in (1.10). The study of more general approximations and of integral representation formulas in the spirit of [1, 22, 23] will be the subject of future investigation. A possible strategy to generalize our result to the case of arbitrary Lamé constants could be to rely on the presence of further discrete-divergence terms, along the lines of [1, 23]. The extension of our compactness result and of our identification of the limiting domain for such a model would be possible without any change to the structure of the current proof. The associated Gamma-convergence analysis, on the other hand, deserves the development of a novel representation theory.

Definitions and notation

Lemma 2.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u :\Omega \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}u:Ω→Rn be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}^{n}$$\end{document}Ln-measurable. Then, for every open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \subset \Omega $$\end{document}U⊂Ω the map2.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (x,\xi ) \mapsto | D\hat{u}^{\xi }_{x}| (U^{\xi }_{x} \setminus J^{1}_{\hat{u}^{\xi }_{x}}) + \mathcal {H}^{0} (U^{\xi }_{x} \cap J^{1}_{\hat{u}^{\xi }_{x}}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(x,ξ)↦|Du^xξ|(Uxξ\Ju^xξ1)+H0(Uxξ∩Ju^xξ1)is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {L}^n \otimes \mathcal {H}^{n-1})$$\end{document}(Ln⊗Hn-1)-measurable on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \times \mathbb {S}^{n-1}$$\end{document}Ω×Sn-1. Next, we recall a measurability property originally stated in [26, Lemma 3.6].

Lemma 2.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v :\Omega \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}v:Ω→R be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}^{n}$$\end{document}Ln-measurable and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi \in \mathbb {S}^{n-1}$$\end{document}ξ∈Sn-1. Then, for every Borel set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B \subset \Omega $$\end{document}B⊂Ω the map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} y \mapsto | Dv^{\xi }_{y}| (B^{\xi }_{y} \setminus J^{1}_{v^{\xi }_{y}}) + \mathcal {H}^{0} (B^{\xi }_{y} \cap J^{1}_{v^{\xi }_{y}}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y↦|Dvyξ|(Byξ\Jvyξ1)+H0(Byξ∩Jvyξ1)is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^{n-1}$$\end{document}Hn-1-measurable on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi ^\xi $$\end{document}Πξ.

We are now in position to define the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GBV}^\mathcal {E}{}(\Omega ;\mathbb {R}^n)$$\end{document}GBVE(Ω;Rn).

Remark 2.5

Observe that the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GBV}^{\mathcal {E}}(\Omega ;\mathbb {R}^n)$$\end{document}GBVE(Ω;Rn) introduced in Definition 2.4 does not coincide a priori with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GBD}(\Omega )$$\end{document}GBD(Ω) introduced in [26, Definition 4.1]. This is because the control in (2.3) is not pointwise in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ but only in average with respect to the -measure. Nevertheless, we will show in Section 3.2 that actually \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GSBV}^{\mathcal {E}}(\Omega ;\mathbb {R}^n)=\text {GSBD}(\Omega )$$\end{document}GSBVE(Ω;Rn)=GSBD(Ω).

In view of the measurability property contained in Lemma 2.3 we provide the following definition.

Definition 2.6

Remark 2.7

Since the hypothesis of De Giorgi-Letta’s Theorem [4] are fulfilled, the extension of the set function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \mapsto \hat{\mu }^\xi _u(U)$$\end{document}U↦μ^uξ(U) to a Borel regular measure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω is uniquely guaranteed by the formula\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\mu }^\xi _u(B) := \inf \{ \hat{\mu }^\xi _{u}(U) : B \subset U, \ U \subset \Omega \text { open} \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ^uξ(B):=inf{μ^uξ(U):B⊂U,U⊂Ωopen}.

Also in this case, by De Giorgi-Letta’s Theorem [4] the extension of the set function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \mapsto \hat{\mu }_{u,p}(U)$$\end{document}U↦μ^u,p(U) to a Borel regular measure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω is uniquely guaranteed by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{aligned} \hat{\mu }_{u,p}(B) := \inf \{ \hat{\mu }_{u,p}(U) : B \subset U, \ U \subset \Omega \text { open} \}. \end{aligned} $$\end{document}μ^u,p(B):=inf{μ^u,p(U):B⊂U,U⊂Ωopen}.The above formula immediately gives also the uniqueness of the extension to a Borel regular measure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω.

We conclude this section with a remark concerning the definition of the functionals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^p_\varepsilon $$\end{document}Fεp as well as the definition of the measures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mu }_{u,p}$$\end{document}μ^u,p.

Remark 2.12

We observe that in (1.4) and (2.4) we can equivalently take the supremum over any family of countable pairwise disjoint open balls and the result would be the same. In addition, all the results as well as their proofs remain unchanged if we replace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}$$\end{document}B with any class made of finitely many pairwise disjoint convex and open sets.

We also remark that the functional (1.4) could be replaced by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {F}'^p_\varepsilon (u,\Omega ):=\sup _{ \mathscr {B}} \sum _{B \in \mathscr {B}}\bigg (\int _{\frac{B-B}{\varepsilon }}F_{\varepsilon ,\xi }(u,B)^p e^{-|\xi |^{2}}\, d\xi \bigg )^\frac{1}{p } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Fε′p(u,Ω):=supB∑B∈B(∫B-BεFε,ξ(u,B)pe-|ξ|2dξ)1pwithout any substantial change in the proofs that follow. In particular, with this choice we would have exactly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}'^1_\varepsilon (u,B)=\mathcal {F}_\varepsilon (u,B)$$\end{document}Fε′1(u,B)=Fε(u,B) for every open ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B \subset \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}B⊂Rn.

Structure of the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GBV}^{\mathcal {E}}$$\end{document}GBVE

The present section addresses the study of the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GBV}^{\mathcal {E}}(\Omega ;\mathbb {R}^n)$$\end{document}GBVE(Ω;Rn) introduced in Definition 2.4. In particular, we focus on the structure of functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in \textrm{GSBV}^{\mathcal {E}}(\Omega ,\mathbb {R}^n)$$\end{document}u∈GSBVE(Ω,Rn). As a byproduct, we prove the following identification Theorem.

Theorem 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subset \mathbb {R}^{n}$$\end{document}Ω⊂Rn open. A measurable function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u :\Omega \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}u:Ω→Rn belongs to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textrm{GSBD}(\Omega )$$\end{document}GSBD(Ω) if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in \textrm{GSBV}^{\mathcal {E}}(\Omega ;\mathbb {R}^n)$$\end{document}u∈GSBVE(Ω;Rn).

Remark 3.2

It is worth noticing that in Theorem 3.1 we do not assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^{n-1}(J_u) < \infty $$\end{document}Hn-1(Ju)<∞.

In the next subsection, we discuss some measurability issues which justify formula (2.3) in Definition 2.4.

Preliminaries

We start with a technical proposition for one-dimensional functions. We introduce the class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T of all functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in C^{1}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}τ∈C1(R) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\frac{1}{2} \le \tau \le \frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}-12≤τ≤12 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \tau '\le 1$$\end{document}0≤τ′≤1. We recall that, given an open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I \subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R and given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau _j) \subset \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in C^1(I)$$\end{document}τ∈C1(I), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _j \rightarrow \tau $$\end{document}τj→τ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1_{loc}(I)$$\end{document}Cloc1(I) if and only if for every open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \Subset \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}j→∞.

Proposition 3.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I \subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R be open and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in \textrm{BV}_{loc}(I)$$\end{document}u∈BVloc(I). Let furthermore \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mathcal {T}} \subset \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T^⊂T be any countable set which is dense with respect to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1_{loc}$$\end{document}Cloc1-topology. For every U open subset of I, it holds3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |Du|(U \setminus J^1_u) + \mathcal {H}^0(U \cap J^1_u) = \sup _{k \in \mathbb {N}} \sup \sum _{i=1}^{k} | D(\tau _{i} (u)) |(U_{i})\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|Du|(U\Ju1)+H0(U∩Ju1)=supk∈Nsup∑i=1k|D(τi(u))|(Ui),where the second supremum is taken over all the families \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _{1}, \ldots \tau _{k} \in \hat{\mathcal {T}}$$\end{document}τ1,…τk∈T^ and all the families of pairwise disjoint open subsets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_{1}, \ldots , U_{k}$$\end{document}U1,…,Uk of U.

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in \textrm{BV}_{loc}(I)$$\end{document}u∈BVloc(I), for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \Subset I$$\end{document}U⋐I open and compactly contained in I, the condition (a) of [26, Theorem 3.5] is satisfied. Therefore, we can apply [26, Theorem 3.8] to the one-dimensional function to deduce that (3.1) holds true for every open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U' \subset U$$\end{document}U′⊂U if we replace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mathcal {T}}$$\end{document}T^ with the entire family \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T. In order to pass from a compactly contained open set U to the whole of I, it is enough to consider a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_k \Subset I$$\end{document}Uk⋐I with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_k \nearrow I$$\end{document}Uk↗I and notice that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |Du|(U_k \setminus J^1_u) + \mathcal {H}^0(U_k \cap J^1_u) \nearrow |Du|(I \setminus J^1_u) + \mathcal {H}^0(I \cap J^1_u), \ \ \text { as }k \rightarrow \infty . $$\end{document}|Du|(Uk\Ju1)+H0(Uk∩Ju1)↗|Du|(I\Ju1)+H0(I∩Ju1),ask→∞.In order to conclude the proof of formula (3.1) we need to show that it is enough to consider the supremum on the smaller family \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mathcal {T}}$$\end{document}T^. To this purpose we simply notice that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \Subset I$$\end{document}U⋐I, being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in \textrm{BV}(U)$$\end{document}u∈BV(U), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert u\Vert _{L^\infty (U)} \le m$$\end{document}‖u‖L∞(U)≤m. Hence, by applying the chain rule for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{BV}$$\end{document}BV-function (see for instance [4]), if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau _j) \subset \hat{\mathcal {T}}$$\end{document}(τj)⊂T^ is such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _j \rightarrow \tau $$\end{document}τj→τ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1_{loc}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Cloc1(R), then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |D(\tau _j(u) - \tau (u))|(U) \le \Vert \tau _j -\tau \Vert _{C^1((-m,m))} \, |Du|(U) \rightarrow 0, \ \ \text { as }j \rightarrow \infty . $$\end{document}|D(τj(u)-τ(u))|(U)≤‖τj-τ‖C1((-m,m))|Du|(U)→0,asj→∞.With this information at hand we infer that the double supremum in the right hand-side of (3.1) does not change when restricted to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mathcal {T}}$$\end{document}T^. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We are now in position to prove Lemma 2.2.

Proof of Lemma 2.2

The assumptions of the lemma are equivalent to: for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^{n-1}$$\end{document}Hn-1-a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi \in \mathbb {S}^{n-1}$$\end{document}ξ∈Sn-1, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}^{n}$$\end{document}Ln-a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \Omega $$\end{document}x∈Ω, the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{u}^{\xi }_{x}$$\end{document}u^xξ belongs to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{BV}_{loc} (\Omega ^{\xi }_{x})$$\end{document}BVloc(Ωxξ).

We claim that for every open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \subset \Omega $$\end{document}U⊂Ω and every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}τ∈T the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,\xi ) \mapsto | D\tau (\hat{u}^\xi _x) |(U^\xi _x)$$\end{document}(x,ξ)↦|Dτ(u^xξ)|(Uxξ) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {L}^n \otimes \mathcal {H}^{n-1})$$\end{document}(Ln⊗Hn-1)-measurable on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \times \mathbb {S}^{n-1}$$\end{document}Ω×Sn-1. To show the claim, consider a countable dense subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D \subset C^1_c(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}D⊂Cc1(R) in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1_{loc}$$\end{document}Cloc1-topology. Notice that, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in L^1_{loc}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈Lloc1(R) and every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I \subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R open, we have3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} | D f |(I) = \sup _{\begin{array}{c} \varphi \in C^1_c(I) \\ \Vert \varphi \Vert _{L^\infty } \le 1 \end{array}} \int _{\mathbb {R}} f \, D\varphi \, \text {d}t = \sup _{\begin{array}{c} \varphi \in C^1_c(I) \cap D \\ \Vert \varphi \Vert _{L^\infty } \le 1 \end{array}} \int _{\mathbb {R}} f \, D\varphi \, \text {d}t. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|Df|(I)=supφ∈Cc1(I)‖φ‖L∞≤1∫RfDφdt=supφ∈Cc1(I)∩D‖φ‖L∞≤1∫RfDφdt.Now consider a sequence of open sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U'_k \Subset U$$\end{document}Uk′⋐U with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U'_k \nearrow U$$\end{document}Uk′↗U, and consider a sequence of functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_k \in C^1_c(U)$$\end{document}vk∈Cc1(U) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le v_k \le 1$$\end{document}0≤vk≤1 while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts}
